Deviation: the allowlist named plan step 5 for UrbanSite; it is done in step 3, since its in-core work is reductions of the stage-start state (the captures) and a damping, the column form above. The reductions are computed in prepare! as a separate read pass of u, v, θ per substage; folding them into the previous substage's sweep (the means of the predicted state) is a later optimisation.

Step 4: the SGS closures inside the sweep (branch issue/533-s4-sgs).

Branch issue/533-s4-sgs (from step 1). What was built:

  • One term for the closure, Operators.SGSTerm (src/operators/sgs_sweep.jl): its prepare! is the compute_sgs! of the separate loops (and qv's own diffusivity, AMD), before the sweep; its row kernel adds the momentum, θ and qv tendencies from the precomputed coefficients. The row kernels (FiniteVolume.EddyMomentum, StressMomentum, EddyScalar, TensorScalar, src/operators/fused_sweep_sgs.jl) run the point bodies of the whole-field operators themselves (_b_eddy_u! …, _b_sgs_stress_div!, _b_stress_div!, _b_eddy_scalar!), writing into the sweep's row buffer through a row adapter (_RowField): one source of truth, and the fused step is bit-identical to one loop per term in every test. The tensor face fluxes became value functions (_tensor_fx/fy/fz) shared by the stored-flux launches and the sweep, which evaluates each interior face twice instead of storing it.
  • Which kernel, from the closure contract: the momentum part from a new generic sgs_momentum_sweep (default: the eddy viscosity of the default add_sgs_momentum!, Laplacian or stress form by sgs_stress; the Reynolds-stress closure its stored stress); θ's from the existing sgs_scalar_face_diffusivity (diagonal) or sgs_scalar_tensor; qv's from a new trait sgs_tracer_own_diffusivity (AMD true), checked at run time against sgs_tracer_diffusivity! (a closure whose two answers disagree stops with an error instead of fusing the wrong flux). SolidMaskedSGS (the urban site's mask) now forwards sgs_scalar_face_diffusivity, sgs_scalar_tensor, sgs_explicit_stress and the two new generics to the closure it wraps.
  • Every closure of the core is fused: Smagorinsky (built-in C_s and StaticSmagorinsky), dynamic (plane-averaged; the local and masked variants go through the same contract defaults but are not tested), Lagrangian dynamic, Vreman, σ, FunctionSGS, MLSGS, AMD, the prognostic TKE closure (also with a solid mask and on a stretched grid), stability-corrected, isentropic tensor diffusion, the Reynolds-stress closure and both SolidMaskedSGS uses; both sgs_stress forms (the stress form, and the Reynolds stress's divergence, on a RegularGrid only, as the operators). builtin.sgs left SWEEP_FALLBACKS; a closure without a sweep form is reported by its type (Main.X: no sweep_term …).
  • The sweep form must match the closure's own tendencies (Operators.sgs_sweep_form_matches, added after the review): the sweep evaluates the kernels the contract generics name, not the closure's add_sgs_momentum!/add_sgs_scalar!/sgs_tracer_diffusivity!. So for each of those, the method dispatch selects of the generic the sweep reads instead (sgs_momentum_sweep; sgs_scalar_face_diffusivity and sgs_scalar_tensor; sgs_tracer_own_diffusivity) must be at least as specific as the tendency's, also for the closure a wrapper forwards to (sgs_sweep_wrapped: IsentropicDiffusion, SolidMaskedSGS); otherwise the closure runs one loop per term and is reported by its type. Cached per closure and simulation type and per world (a method defined later is seen); the step's allocation is unchanged (32 and 48 bytes).
  • Footprint (1, 1, 1): with νt precomputed, every SGS body reads the state at most one point away (the compact flux differences; the tensor's cross terms at the diagonal neighbours); νt, κh, the tensor and a stored stress are inputs the predictor never overwrites, so they do not enter the lag. The ring and edges stay those of the base term (2 rows, 1 plane); the reversed-slab tests check the front edge.
  • The decision receives the closure with its sgs_stress (Simulations.SGSChoice), so the decision functions of step 1 keep their signatures. _sweep_terms infers a two-type union; a fused step_rk3! with Smagorinsky or AMD allocates what one loop per term does (32 and 48 bytes, the keyword call).

Not done: the masked scalar diffusion of plan step 4. The plan names "the prognostic TKE closure (PrognosticSGS), including the masked scalar diffusion". What this branch fuses is the TKE closure's tendencies of u, v, w, θ and qv (mask included). Not inside the sweep: the TKE equation of TKEClosure (and the stress transport of the Reynolds-stress closure) runs in advance_sgs!, once per step after the projection, on its own field e, which the sweep's buffer does not carry; its masked diffusion (add_eddy_diffusion_scalar!(…; mask)) is part of that update (already one plane loop, KA-l). The urban tracers' masked SGS diffusion (TracerSGSDiffusion) is a component outside the core. Whether either has to move into the sweep for step 4 to count as done is left to the maintainer.

Tests (test/fused_sweep_sgs.jl, 5656 assertions, 80–101 s alone and 284 s beside six other files, so tagged heavy in test/test_tags.toml): the sweep over (base, SGS term) against the separate loops + predictor for the 15 closures above, both stress forms, 1–5 slabs forward and reversed, serial and threaded, Euler and the RK3 combination, dry and moist, periodic, walls and stretched (within ROUNDOFFRTOL; measured bit-identical); step!/`steprk3!withloopfusion = "always"against"never"for every closure; a closure without a sweep form reported and refused by"always"; the stress form on a stretched grid keeps the operator's error; footprint, inference, allocation; a guard that every closure type of Aeolus with its ownaddsgsmomentum!,addsgsscalar!orsgstracerdiffusivity!method has the matching sweep generic; and, at run time, closures defined in the test (own θ flux, own momentum, own tracer diffusivity, the θ one insideSolidMaskedSGS) stay out of the sweep, are reported by type and agree with"never", one with the matching face form fuses, a sweep method defined later is picked up, and the Reynolds stress has no sweep form on a stretched grid.test/fusedsweep.jl:builtin.sgs` removed from its expectations.

Bugs the tests caught (code wrong): the first SGSTerm boxed its parts per substage (1.5 kB per step_rk3!): the momentum part inferred as Symbol on the stress check's path, and qv's part depended on the run's moisture; fixed (see above). Expectations that proved wrong: the stress form on a stretched grid was in the first agreement matrix (the operator refuses it; it is now tested as an error); "the Reynolds stress acted" failed on its initial isotropic stress, which has no divergence (now three committed steps first, and θ's anomaly compared). Bugs the review caught (code wrong): nothing checked that the sweep's kernels are the closure's own; a closure from outside Aeolus that overrides add_sgs_scalar! without a face form (like the validation fixture BackgroundGradient, which adds a background θ flux) would have fused with the default diagonal kernel and silently dropped that flux. Latent in the repository (that run also passes a plain-function body force, which keeps it off the sweep); fixed by sgs_sweep_form_matches (above). The Reynolds stress's sweep form did not refuse a stretched grid (shadowed by its tensor's refusal); it now does.

Measured (Ryzen AI 9 HX 370, 15 W cap, under the benchmark lock). On this branch alone no configured case with SGS fuses: the dry CBL still needs the pointwise terms (step 2) and the budgets (step 7), GABLS1 and GABLS3 also LargeScale (step 3) and SurfaceLayer (steps 5, 6). The fused step agrees with loop_fusion = "never" after 10 steps (max |Δ| of u, w, θ: 0). bench_roofline.jl config … 2 8 with ROOFLINE_PROF=0 ROOFLINE_ALTERNATE=1 (each step alternates the two settings in one process), min of 8 steps of each, ms per step; the thread counts run in the order 1, 2, 6, 12, one process each:

Full suite on this branch: 118 jobs, 10894 assertions, 0 failed; validation gabls1.jl 8/8, gabls3.jl 19/19. Bytes (profiled runs, 128 × 128 × 64, one Euler substage per step, the compulsory model of bench_roofline.jl, the FFT's pencil items left out): 547 MB per step (521 B/point) with "never", 218 MB (208 B/point) fused; time in launches 29.0 → 19.7 ms at 1 thread, 18.5 → 9.5 ms at 6. GABLS1 and GABLS3 not measured fused (above); they run one loop per term as before; their validation runs on this branch pass (validation/run.jl --only gabls1.jl,gabls3.jl: 8 and 19 checks, 104 s and 83 s).

Plan step 9 (recompute νt in the sweep) would not pay on this evidence. The coefficient pass (_b_smagorinsky!, reads u, v, w, writes νt) is 33.6 MB = 32 B/point, 15 % of the fused step's compulsory bytes; with the sweep's own read of νt, recomputing would remove at most 40 B/point (19 %). But the pass is not at the bandwidth roof: 5.2 ms at 1 thread (6.4 GB/s, against a DRAM triad of 43 GB/s) and 1.55 ms at 6 (21.7 GB/s, against 52.6), so its time is mostly the strain rate's arithmetic, which recomputing keeps (and multiplies, unless the strain goes into a rolling plane buffer and the term's footprint grows to 2). Its bytes at the roof are 0.64 ms + 0.16 ms (the νt read) of the 13.0 ms fused step at 6 threads, about 6 %, and nothing measurable at 1–2 threads. Worth revisiting only if a closure's coefficient pass is found at the roof (the dynamic and AMD closures, Berlin at 12–24 threads).

Step 5: near-wall terms, nest registers and open faces (branch issue/533-s5-nearwall-open). What was built:

  • Open lateral faces no longer keep a substage off the fused sweep (builtin.open_boundaries removed from SWEEP_FALLBACKS). Nothing had to change in the sweep: the boundary update (the data, the radiating outflow from the stage-start layers mark_open_boundary_stage! records, the mass correction) already ran after the sweep and before the projection in _blocked_projection!; the sweep updates the same interior points as the separate axpy! passes, and the update reads only the velocity and the stage-start layers, so moving θ's update before it changes nothing. Verified: step!/step_rk3! with loop_fusion = "always" against "never", Dirichlet, Orlanski and convective outflow, regular and stretched grids, dry and moist, serial and threaded: bit-identical in every case tried (the test asserts round-off, agree).
  • SurfaceLayer: its first-level heat flux is a sweep term, Components.SurfaceHeatFluxTerm (tθ += wθ[i, j]/Δz on the first level, wθ the step's per-column solve as input; a row kernel that returns at once off the first level). Its implicit wall stress is a post-predictor operator (plan step 6), so its allowlist entry stays with that reason only, and a domain with it is still unfused.
  • Immersed walls / Urban.WallModelFacades: the removal of the stencil's flux through the walls (remove_stencil_wall_flux!) is a sweep term, WallModels.StencilWallFluxTerm. The fluxes depend only on the stage-start velocity and ν_t, so its prepare! (stencil_wall_fluxes!, one work item per pair, the force folded as before into stats[:F_stencil]) computes them before the sweep; each row subtracts the fluxes of its pairs, grouped by storage row once (wall_rows, cached in the component), a face's pairs in pair order: the reference's operations in the same order. The flux expression is one function (_iw_pair_x) shared by the separate pass and the sweep. The facade stress itself is post-predictor (step 6): the entry stays with that reason.
  • IBM penalty (IBM.IBMPenaltyTerm): the row body of the penalty launch, _ibm_row_body!, writes exactly the faces of its storage row, so the sweep runs it unchanged on its tendency buffer (_RowOut, a view that maps out[I, J, K] to the row buffer); τ = penalty_time × Δt is set in prepare! through a slot the component keeps. SimulationCouplings uses it when the obstacles are its only in-core part; with a slab its floor flux (plan step 2's surface-flux term) keeps it unfused, and its entry names only that.
  • Nest flux registers: the register hooks (Nesting._RegisterHook, _HookChain) and Urban.NestingCoupling's :body_force book the stage's interface fluxes from the stage-start state and add no tendency, so the hooks' term (Nesting.RegisterBookingTerm) does the booking in prepare! and nothing in the rows. A two-way nest (16³ parent, 16³ child, no SGS) runs fused in both domains, agrees with the unfused nest, and conserves the composite ∫θ to 4.2e-15 (relative, 4 steps), the same as unfused. Urban.NestingCoupling keeps its allowlist entry: every urban domain also carries Urban.UrbanSite's :tendencies stage, which is still unfused, so a term for the coupling could not run in any configuration nor be tested. A first version removed the entry with an untested Urban.NestFluxBookingTerm; the review caught it and the term was withdrawn until UrbanSite has its sweep term.
  • Regular grids only, as the whole-field references: SolidMask (the mask of the IBM penalty and of the immersed walls) exists on a RegularGrid only, and the walls' spacings and the surface layer's first-level flux divide by the uniform mesh.dz. On a z-stretched mesh the surface-layer term and its stage both throw before any stepping; a test asserts the refusal.

Tests: test/fused_sweep_nearwall.jl (634 assertions): each term and all three composed through blocked_predictor! against base tendencies + the term's whole-field pass + predictor at 1-5 slabs (also in reverse slab order), serial and threaded, dry and moist, Euler and the RK3 stage combination; step!/step_rk3! of a domain with the surface layer's tendency part, a wall-flux component and SimulationCouplings with obstacles, fused = unfused; the facade wall model's adapter on the synthetic city (small_city.toml) against its stage; the refusal on a stretched mesh; @inferred term construction; allocation (below). All comparisons at ROUNDOFFRTOL (1e-10); all measured bit-identical. Also run with -t 4 (real slabs): green. The completeness guard in `test/fusedsweep.jlnow also capturesupdateopenboundaries!` and checks that it sits after the predictor/sweep and before the projection (the review found that its map entry had never been matched).

Allocation: prepare! allocates 32 bytes (the store of stats[:F_stencil]). On a 20 × 12 × 16 grid with one thread the composed sweep allocates nothing more (the test bound is 1024 bytes). With -t 4 on 64 × 64 × 32 (review measurement) blocked_predictor! with base + surface flux + wall flux + IBM penalty allocated 3456 B (1 slab) and 11504 B (4 slabs), against 1216 B and 7024 B for the base terms alone: the task closures of the threaded launch, a constant per call, not per point or per wall.

Bug the tests caught (the code was wrong): the completeness guard looks up sweep_term(::C, ::Any); the first urban methods were typed d::Domain, so the guard did not see NestingCoupling's term. They now take an untyped domain, as the step-1 methods do.

Measured: step_rk3! at 128 × 128 × 64, loop_fusion = "never" (what these cases ran before this step) and "always" built in the same process and interleaved, 5 rounds of 6 steps each after 2 warm-up steps, one process per thread count under the benchmark lock (short runs, not the sustained-power protocol of #535; 1-minute load at start 2.6–5.7 from other agents' unlocked jobs), s/step, minimum (median):

The last row is the full SurfaceLayer component, unfused (its wall stress keeps it off the sweep), so it belongs in neither column as a fused number (an earlier draft put it in the fused column); it is the fair baseline for the surface-layer case. The test wrapper that runs only the tendency part unfused was systematically 3–7 % slower than the full unfused component, which does strictly more work, in all four 12-thread runs (implementer, review, two re-runs during the review fixes; s/step min, wrapper unfused / full unfused / fused tendency part): 0.156 / 0.150 / 0.146, 0.138 / 0.134 / 0.127, 0.162 / 0.151 / 0.145, 0.142 / 0.138 / 0.129. So about half of the apparent gain is the wrapper. Against the full unfused component the fused tendency part is 3–7 % faster at 12 threads; at 1 thread it ranges from 2 % slower to 4 % faster (full unfused / fused: 0.156 / 0.153, 0.195 / 0.198, 0.146 / 0.140), within the noise.

For the open box with FunctionBoundaryData the fused step was 1–13 % faster at 12 threads in the three runs that timed both, and equal within noise at 1 thread. The 1- and 12-thread numbers do not reproduce between runs, so no scaling statement is made from them: with FunctionBoundaryData the whole step measured 0.171 (1 thread) vs 0.213 (12 threads) in the implementer's run, 0.169 vs 0.284 in the review's run (1-minute load about 20 during the 12-thread part), 0.210 vs 0.195 and 0.178 vs 0.171 in the two re-runs (load 2–4), all "never". update_open_boundaries! alone, with FunctionBoundaryData, measured 0.0137–0.0193 s per call at 1 thread and 0.0152–0.0352 s at 12 threads (three calls per RK3 step); in the re-runs at load 2–4 it did not get faster with threads (0.0142 vs 0.0152 s). The surface layer's per-column MOST solve (stage 3) is a serial loop. Neither is one of this issue's terms; both are listed as open problems. The full SurfaceLayer component stays unfused until plan step 6 (its wall stress), so a GABLS-type case gains nothing from this step alone.

Not done here: Urban.UrbanSite's :tendencies (the captures of the top and open-face heat, a reduction, and the open faces' θ relaxation, a ReferenceDamping term that steps 2 and 3 build) keeps its entry; the slab's floor flux of SimulationCouplings waits for step 2's surface-flux term; the post-predictor parts of SurfaceLayer and WallModelFacades are step 6. So no configured near-wall case (GABLS1, the urban site) is fused yet by this step alone; the open box and a nest without SGS are. Bytes per point against bench_roofline.jl, 2/6/24 threads and the sustained-power protocol were not measured. Full suite green on the final branch (118 jobs, 5886 assertions). Validation: urban_checks.jl 33/33 and gabls1.jl 8/8 (the urban site is not fused by this step; its run is unchanged).

Step 6: post-predictor operators inside the sweep (branch issue/533-s6-post-predictor, from issue/533-sweep-interface, with #538 / PR #543 merged in so the tiled surface's drag is built on its order-independent Jacobi form). What was built:

  • The interface (src/operators/fused_sweep.jl): PostTerm, built by post_sweep_term(x, ctx, Δt) (nothing = not expressible), with footprint, prepare!, finish! and the row kernel post_row!(t, Val(1|2), J, K, I1, I2, st): phase 1 computes the factors of a row from values no face of the operator has changed yet, phase 2 applies them (Jacobi, as the whole-field operators). AfterSweep(f) is an operator that needs the whole predicted field: f() runs after the sweep, and so does every operator after it.
  • The driver: a new blocked_predictor!(bufs, terms, post, halo!, …) method (the old signature calls it with no post terms). A footprint-0 term runs on a row right after its predictor; a term of y-radius r computes factors r rows and applies them 2r rows behind its input (post_plan: per term the factor and apply widths in x, y, z and the two delays, growing by each term's radius). Points whose stencil reaches a halo, a slab's edge planes or a point an earlier term deferred run after the sweep in the same two phases, with the velocity halos filled (_post_deferred!). The slab loop is one loop over t in j1 : j2 + lag + post_delay.
  • The model's operators as post terms, each against its existing whole-field method: KinematicFlowTerm (radius 0), IBMCorrectionTerm (radius 0; a face scaled once per adjacent solid cell, the boundary faces in finish!), CanopyDragTerm (PlantCanopy, footprint 1), ImmersedWallTerm (facades, footprint 1), WallStressTerm (bottom wall, local formulation; wrapped by SurfaceLayerPostTerm and by the urban ground's GroundStressTerm) and TiledDragTerm (#538's kernels). The sparse face lists get row pointers (row_index, compressed sparse rows) and a row's x-range is found by bisection (row_entries(ptr, r, x, I1, I2)). The ground stress runs as an AfterSweep for TBLE, non-local formulations or an unprimed matching filter; the facades when their pairs are not sorted.
  • Removed from SWEEP_FALLBACKS: Components.KinematicFlow, Urban.VegDrag, Urban.IBMCoupling, Urban.SurfaceExchangeMOST, Urban.TiledUrbanSurface. builtin.post_predictor now names only a post_predictor! passed to the core as a plain function; the SurfaceLayer and WallModelFacades entries keep only their tendency parts (plan step 5). The completeness guard (test/fused_sweep.jl) checks per stage.

Measured (benchmarks/bench_post_sweep.jl 128 64 15: 128² × 64 cells, 16 buildings, 26 368 facade pairs, 150 528 canopy faces; one Euler substage of advection + diffusion + predictor + the IBM correction, facade and ground wall stress and canopy drag; ms per substage, median of 15 calls interleaved call by call between the four forms within one process, each process about a minute, under the benchmark lock; Ryzen AI 9 HX 370 at the 15 W cap):

threads12612
one loop per term11.429.497.987.03

sweep, all four on its rows13.9911.349.598.56

The row forms of the operators with a stencil are slower than their whole-field passes: per operator (1 / 12 threads, increase over the sweep alone against the whole-field pass) canopy drag 4.4 / 2.9 ms against 2.1 / 1.3 ms, facades 2.6 / 2.0 against 1.5 / 1.4, ground stress 1.2 / 0.8 against 0.7 / 0.6 (ratio 1.3–2.2× at 12 threads). These per-operator numbers come from scratch runs (the committed benchmark times only the four forms of the table), as does the bisection figure below. The whole-field passes walk a sparse face list in one launch; on the rows the same faces are found per row, and at many slabs most of them fall in the deferred bands (12 slabs of 5–6 planes). The pointwise IBM correction on the rows costs what its pass costs (0.5 against 0.7 ms at 1 thread, 0.3 against 0.4 at 12, in single- operator runs). Hence the last row: a domain keeps footprint-0 terms on the rows and runs a component whose term has a stencil through its own run_stage! after the sweep (Components._in_rows_or_after, and Components.post_after_sweep for the components known to have a stencil, so their row form is not built). Against one loop per term, the domain's mix is 11 % (1 thread) to 24 % (12 threads) faster per substage of tendencies + predictor + post-predictor operators, without the pressure projection, in this synthetic setup with the urban operators — which in a real run stay unfused while the urban site books budgets (deviation 2). The gain per step is smaller (the projection is not in it), and no configured case was measured: the configurations this step does make fusable today are domains with KinematicFlow (on the rows) or TiledUrbanSurface (after the sweep), and SurfaceLayer once plan step 5 lands. The first row-form version (bisection per row, the whole row scanned for each x-range) was slower still; the row pointers and the bisection of the x-range took the 12-thread canopy increase from 4.0 to 2.9 ms. No allocation: blocked_predictor! with six post terms allocates 0 bytes serial and at most 64 kB threaded (the tasks). Not measured: bytes per step, 24 threads, the configured cases (the urban site stays unfused, below).

Tests (test/fused_sweep_post.jl, 1538 assertions): the driver with synthetic radius-0 and radius-1 Jacobi terms, chained and with an AfterSweep, against predictor + whole-field passes at 1–5 slabs, the slabs also in reverse order, serial and threaded, regular and stretched grids, dry and moist, Euler and RK3 stages (round-off tolerance); each model operator in the sweep against its whole-field method, diagnostics included (iw.stats, the ground's u_star, the canopy force, the surface layer's stress sums); domains with KinematicFlow and tiles, loop_fusion = "always" against "never" over 3 steps; the small-city urban site's post-predictor hook against the sweep with its couplings (row forms and the domain's mix, 1 and 3 slabs); the row pointers and bisection against a filter; type stability and allocation. Test expectation that proved wrong: the first surface-layer comparison called the reference and the sweep twice without refilling the halos between, so the second call compared against stale halos; a substage refills them (step 1), and the test now does. The code was right.

Bugs the review caught (each with a test in fused_sweep_post.jl): (1) the domain built every post-predictor component's row form each substage before deciding to run its run_stage! after the sweep; for the wall stresses that is three Nx × Ny arrays and the face lists per substage (0.19–0.36 ms and about 860 kB at 128² × 64, 1 thread, 2–3.5 % of the substage). Components.post_after_sweep(c) now decides first; the test asserts the hook allocates less than one Nx × Ny plane where the row form allocates more. Latent until plan step 5 (the surface layer) or step 7 (budgets) lands. (2) With budgets on, both hooks named builtin.budgets, and the report listed it twice; the blocker list is now de-duplicated.

Deviations: (1) the operators with a stencil run after the sweep in a domain, by the measurement above, not on the delayed rows. Their row forms (CanopyDragTerm, ImmersedWallTerm, WallStressTerm / SurfaceLayerPostTerm, GroundStressTerm, TiledDragTerm) are tested but no configuration runs them; on the rows run only KinematicFlowTerm and IBMCorrectionTerm. Removing Urban.VegDrag, Urban.SurfaceExchangeMOST and Urban.TiledUrbanSurface from SWEEP_FALLBACKS means their substage stays fused with a whole-field pass after the sweep, not that they run on the rows. (2) The urban site always books budgets, so its post-predictor hook is not fused until plan step 7 (post_hook_terms returns nothing with budgets); its couplings are tested at term level. (3) A term on the rows is not timed through UrbanEntry (only its AfterSweep is). (4) Post terms do not read θ or qv: the sweep advances the scalars before them, the whole-field order after.

Step 7: budgets inside the sweep (branch issue/533-s7-budgets, on top of step 1). Budgets on no longer force one loop per term (builtin.budgets left the allowlist). What was built:

  • Booked terms (src/operators/fused_sweep_budgets.jl): BookedTerm(term, sums, slot) wraps the sweep term of an attributed in-core component. It adds exactly what its term adds; per row it sums each booked field of the tendency buffer (u, v, θ, qv: the core tendencies of the momentum, energy and water budgets) over i before and after the term's add_row! (air cells only where the budget's meter leaves solid cells out) and adds the difference to LevelSums.acc[f, slot, k]. The rows are in L1, so this moves no DRAM bytes. No reduction across threads is needed: every row (j, k) of a slab, edge planes included, is computed by that slab's task, so each level's sum has one writer, and its summation order (i in a row, rows in j order) does not depend on the slab count.
  • finish_sweep!(t, sim, Δt), a new optional method of the term interface, called after the sweep and before the projection (default nothing).
  • The budget term (Components.SweepBudget, components/budgets.jl): with the budgets on, hook_terms returns (SweepBudget, terms…), the attributed components' terms wrapped as BookedTerms (an entry with attributed(c, Val(stage)) = false stays unwrapped and its effect stays in the dynamics' share, as before). Its prepare! counts the substage (the hook's _count_substage!, so the RK3 weights and the discard of a retried step work unchanged) and zeroes the sums. Its finish_sweep! books weight × Δt × w_k × acc per component and quantity through the new BudgetSystem.book_context!. That function does what budget_open! … budget_close! of a nested tendency context does with a change measured elsewhere: it counts as booked for the dynamics' context, and it is deferred while that context is open. It is built once per domain (cached in DomainBudgets.sweep); hook_terms stays @inferred and allocates 0 bytes.
  • Compatible with #539 (PR #541, not merged): the sweep books every quantity whose core tendency it holds. For a quantity a component's budget_writes leaves out, the per-level sums are exactly 0 and nothing is booked, the same outcome as #541's skipped nested context. At integration: the extra verify field of DomainBudgets next to sweep, and book_context! may set #541's changed flag. The sums read the meter's mask through !iszero / Bool, so they work with both #541's one-byte mask and the Float64 mask on this base.

Tests (test/fused_sweep_budgets.jl, 657 assertions):

  • The level sums of a pointwise term (u, v, w, θ, qv, with a heating profile) and of a radius-2 term (3-row ring, 2 edge planes) match the per-level sums of the terms' whole-field contributions, for 1–5 slabs (also in reverse slab order), serial and threaded, regular and stretched grids, dry and moist, θ and qv masked by a random solid mask. Largest relative difference: 8.6e-12 (tolerance 1e-10, round-off of the before/after cancellation, eps × Σ|t|). Booking does not change the state.
  • A domain with budgets on (energy, momentumx, momentumy; water when moist) and three in-core components, one of them not attributed, runs fully fused (loop_fusion = "always"). Its budgets agree term by term with the same domain on one loop per term ("never", the reference contexts on the whole tendency fields). This holds for Euler and RK3, regular and stretched grids, dry and moist. Largest difference: 2.2e-14 of the largest term.
  • The closure stays within the existing 1e-12 of the largest term. The attributed heating equals the analytic ρ₀ cp Σk Qk Δzk n_air,k/(Nx Ny) t to 1e-12. The fused run makes exactly 2 passes fewer per attributed component, quantity and substage.
  • A retried adaptive step (a term whose prepare! throws on the first attempt) books its fused in-core term once.
  • The sweep with booked terms allocates 0 bytes.
  • In test/fused_sweep.jl, the step-1 assertion "budgets on → reported builtin.budgets" is gone; it was expected to go in this step.
  • Targeted suites green: fusedsweep, fusedsweepbudgets, budgetsoutput, componentsbothcores, configsimulationdriver, modelnesting, nesttwoway, urbanrun, urbanchecks (1883 assertions).

Measured (bench_budgets.jl, a scratch script). The case is synthetic: 128³ periodic Boussinesq RK3, centred advection, and one :body_force component with a sweep term (pointwise damping of u, v, θ). Its buoyancy is switched off, because no real component or built-in term other than the base has a fused form on this branch (plan steps 2–6). Budgets: energy, momentumx, momentumy. The variants were interleaved in one process, 4 steps × 6 repetitions, minimum s/step, under the benchmark lock. Other agents' unlocked jobs kept the 1-minute load at 4–8 and the power cap (#535) applies, so the absolute 12-thread numbers moved by up to 2× between processes; compare only within a row.

  • Budget passes per step: 9 fused (one per quantity at the step's start, the dynamics' close and the step's end), against 27 on one loop per term (+2 per in-core component, quantity and substage). The modelled bytes of those passes on this base (field + Float64 mask, 16 B/pt per pass) are 144 against 432 B/pt per step, and half that with #541's mask. The booking itself adds no DRAM traffic.
  • Split at 1 thread (profile of 15 fused steps, budgets on): the meter passes take 4.3 % of the step and the row sums of the booked term 3.3 %. Micro-benchmark of one 128³ sweep: 15.4 → 16.1–17.7 ms with booking at 1 thread (+4–14 %), 4.0–4.3 → 4.3–4.4 ms at 12 threads (+2–8 %); one meter pass is 0.4–0.7 ms.
  • Result: with loop fusion, budgets on cost from noise level up to 5 % of the step at 12 threads (+0.6, −2.3, +4.3, +4.6 % in runs a–d) and 3–12 % at 1 thread. On one loop per term they cost 10–25 %.
  • Not measured: Berlin and a configured non-urban case (they do not run fused before plan steps 2–6), 2/6/24 threads, and bytes per step against bench_roofline.jl.

Bug the review caught: the budget term decided its booked slots by sweep_term when it was first built, and it is built (and cached) at the first substage that asks for the sweep's terms, also an unfused one. A component whose sweep term appeared only later was summed in the sweep but never booked, its share falling silently into the dynamics' term with the closure still round-off. No component behaves like this today (every sweep_term is static). Now every attributed in-core entry has a booked slot; one whose component added nothing sums to 0 and books nothing. Regression test: "a sweep term that appears after the budget term was built is booked" (it fails, 4 assertions, without the fix).

Deviations:

  • The issue's "per-thread, per-level partial sums reduced after the sweep" became per-level sums with one writer each, because the slabs own their levels. The GPU per-point form (plan step 8) will need real per-thread partial sums.
  • A row sums the whole booked row before and after each booked term rather than the per-point difference. This works for row-form terms too, at the price of one cancellation per row, which is no worse than the reference's per-level cancellation.
  • Possible later saving: adjacent booked terms could reuse the previous term's "after" sums as their "before" sums, halving the row sums.

Step 8: the other step cores and the GPU form (branch issue/533-s8-other-cores, on top of step 1). What was built:

  • The sound-proof cores (src/anelastic/fused_sweep.jl): step_anelastic!, step_pseudo_incompressible! and their step_rk3! run steps 2-7 and 11 of _mass_weighted_step! in the fused sweep. The base term is Anelastic.WeightedBaseTendencies (the W-weighted flux-form advection + ν∇²), the buoyancy g(θ − θ̄)/θ̄ is Anelastic.AnelasticBuoyancy (footprint (0, 0, 1)), components come through the body-force hook as in the Boussinesq core. The point functions of weighted_momentum_advect!, weighted_scalar_advect! and add_anelastic_buoyancy! were split out of their kernel bodies (_wmom_u …, _anelastic_buoyancy), so the passes and the sweep share one expression each. Coriolis, surface flux, sponge and SGS of these cores are reported with their own keys (builtin.anelastic_*): the step-2/4 terms are built for the Boussinesq core and still have to be wired in here.
  • The explicit part of step_imex!: one fused tendency launch (FiniteVolume.term_tendencies!, the ring sweep's row kernels writing sim.tu … tqv) when every term of _explicit_tendencies! is a sweep term; the term tuple is the Boussinesq core's (_sweep_terms with no closure, no post-predictor and no diffusion). No predictor in the launch: the stage combinations and the implicit solves read the tendencies, so this is loop fusion of the terms only. The explicit SGS halves (sgs_stress = :symmetric, a scalar diffusivity tensor) are reported (builtin.imex_sgs); with the Laplacian SGS (implicit) the explicit part is fused.
  • The GPU form: Backends.launch_points! (one KA work item per point on any KA backend; all KA kernels stay in Backends.jl) and FiniteVolume.pointwise_predictor!: kernel 1 evaluates the composed term tuple per point (init_point! of the base term, then every add_tendency!) into the tendency fields (FiniteVolume.TendencyFields, the fields seen as the sweep's five-index buffer), kernel 2 the predictor. Simulations.fused_predictor! picks the ring sweep on a CPU backend and the per-point form otherwise; the per-point form also runs on KernelAbstractions.CPU() when sim.workspaces.fused.per_point is set, the stand-in the tests use (no GPU here).
  • Interface: BaseTerm <: SweepTerm for the row-initialising terms (init_row!, init_point!; blocked_predictor! takes Tuple{BaseTerm, Vararg{SweepTerm}}); add_tendency! is now required of every term (the per-point form calls it; add_row! stays an optional CPU row form). builtin.backend and builtin.core left SWEEP_FALLBACKS; set_loop_fusion! accepts "always" on every core.

Tests (test/fused_sweep_cores.jl, 486 assertions): the per-point form against the ring sweep (1-5 slabs, serial and threaded, regular and stretched, dry and moist, Euler and the RK3 combination, with a pointwise and a radius-2 test term); step!/step_rk3! in the per-point form against the ring sweep; IMEX ARK2 and Rosenbrock2 fused against "never" (dry, moist, three backends, per point), with a component's term through the hook; the sound-proof base term and buoyancy in the sweep against their whole-field passes at 1-5 slabs and in the per-point form; the three sound-proof drivers fused against "never" (ν on/off, buoyancy on/off, three backends, per point, and through an anelastic Domain), (1/W)∇·(Wu) < 1e-12 after the Euler steps (measured ~1e-17 s⁻¹); every blocker reported and refused under "always"; allocation (every step core, fused or unfused under "if_possible", allocates no more than under "never"; on the serial backend, or on the test grid where the launches stay below the threading threshold, that is 0 B per step! and step_anelastic!, 704 B per anelastic step_rk3!, 0 B per term_tendencies!; threaded, each launch allocates its task closures, fewer fused: anelastic 128 × 128 × 64 at 6 threads, 77 kB fused against 133 kB per step_anelastic!, 233 kB against 400 kB per step_rk3!); and two guards: every SweepTerm in Aeolus has the point kernel the per-point form needs, and every built-in term of _mass_weighted_step! and of _explicit_tendencies! is in the sweep or documented. All agreements are within ROUNDOFF_RTOL and measured bit-identical (the same expressions in the same order).

A test expectation that was wrong: the first version asserted ∇·(ρ̄u) < 1e-9 after every sound-proof driver. It failed for step_rk3! (its combination with the seeded, unprojected uⁿ is not constrained; the unfused reference gives the same 1.8e-3) and for the pseudo-incompressible core (whose constraint is on ρ̄θ̄u, not ρ̄u). The code was right; the check is now the core's own constraint after the Euler steps.

Bugs the review caught (code wrong, now tested in "no allocation added by the fused or the unfused report, any step core"): the unfused report built its blocker closure on every unfused substage, and Julia boxed it (+16 B per step!, +32 B per step_rk3! with a term without a fused form; the base allocated 0 B there); the blockers are now built from the passed-through arguments inside the @noinline reporter. The fused IMEX launch passed a Union-typed qv and backend on, which were boxed (+176 B per stage, +352 B per ARK2 step over "never"); concrete arguments through a function barrier. Both measured equal to "never" after the fix.

Steps 3, 6, 7 and 8 integrated (branch issue/533-integration, on top of step 1; steps 2, 5 and 4 followed, see "Steps 2, 5 and 4 added" below). Merge order, interface-changing steps first: step 8 (#548: BaseTerm, add_tendency! required of every term), step 6 (#549: post terms), step 7 (#546: finish_sweep!, the budget term in the hook's tuple), step 3 (#547). Textual conflicts (allowlist, imports, API page, runtests.jl, these sub-sections) were resolved as unions; SWEEP_FALLBACKS holds the union of the removals. Conflicts between the steps' intent, each found by a test and fixed here:

  • Per-point form × post terms (6 × 8): fused_predictor! gains the post terms; the per-point form runs them after its predictor on every point (new pointwise_predictor! method, the schedule post_plan gives a point outside the sweep's widths). Tested against predictor + whole-field passes and a KinematicFlow/tiles domain against "never".
  • Sound-proof cores × post terms (6 × 8): those cores have no post terms; with step 6 the pipeline's post hook names no blocker, so a new key builtin.anelastic_post_predictor says why such a substage is unfused.
  • Post hook × budgets (6 × 7): step 6 refused a post hook with budgets and named builtin.budgets, which step 7 removed. An attributed post-predictor component now runs in its budget context as an AfterSweep, so the substage stays fused (bookings against "never" within 1e-10 of the largest term). The urban site, which always books budgets, is therefore no longer kept out by its post hook.
  • Point kernels (3, 7 × 8): step 8's guard requires add_tendency! of every SweepTerm; LargeScaleTerm, UrbanSiteTerm (new LargeScaleForcing.add_column_point!, the same expression per point), BookedTerm and SweepBudget get one. The per-point form has no per-level sums (a level is shared by many work items), so a tuple that books stays unfused there (builtin.budgets_per_point).
  • Budgets in the other cores (7 × 8): the sound-proof cores and the fused IMEX launch never called finish_sweep!, so their fused in-core bookings were dropped, and _build_sweep_budget read sim.has_moisture, which the sound-proof cores lack (FieldError). Fixed; an anelastic domain with budgets, fused against "never", is tested.

Plan step 9 (recompute νt inside the sweep): from the bytes above, the νt chain is _n2_body! (θ, qv → N², 24 B/point) and _coefficients_body! (e, N², lwall → νt, κt, 40 B/point), 4 calls per step, 0.61 GB/step; a step-4 sweep reads νt, κt as inputs (16 B/point per substage). Recomputing them inside the sweep would replace this with the e and lwall reads, saving at most ~190 B/point per step (~5 % of 3629), ~15 ms/step at the triad, while recomputing c_k l √e and the stability length 4-6 times per point for the face averages of the eddy stencils. Not worth it now: the budget sums (22 %, #539) and steps 2, 4, 5 (which make Berlin fusable at all) come first; revisit after step 4 is measured.

Steps 2, 5 and 4 added (2026-11-10, for the HPC benchmark #566; merge commits 11b4c255, ab30c1d0, b42068dd, in that order). Textual conflicts as unions; SWEEP_FALLBACKS takes every step's removals (builtin.sgs, builtin.open_boundaries, Coriolis, the surface θ flux, sponge, fringe, moist buoyancy, SurfaceLayer, WallModelFacades, SimulationCouplings gone). The plan of a Boussinesq substage is (base, SGS, built-in pointwise terms, hook terms), the order of the separate passes. Conflicts between the steps' intent, each with a test:

  • Component terms × budgets (2 × 7): a component's term is OptionalTerm(on, BookedTerm(term)); OptionalTerm forwards finish_sweep! and books (by type, so the per-point refusal does not depend on a run-time switch). fused_sweep_budgets.jl.
  • Point kernels (2, 4, 5 × 8): step 2's built-in OptionalTerms are in every Boussinesq plan, so a fallback for terms without add_tendency! would have unfused every per-point substage. Point kernels instead, each the row form's arithmetic at one point: SurfaceFluxTerm, DampingTerm, OptionalTerm, FringeSweep, SpongeSweep, GeostrophicSweep, MoistBuoyancySweep (step 2); IBMPenaltyTerm, StencilWallFluxTerm, RegisterBookingTerm (step 5); SGSTerm with the parts' point forms add_part_point! / add_scalar_point! (step 4). Tested in the per-point form against "never" (fused_sweep_pointwise.jl, fused_sweep_nearwall.jl, fused_sweep_sgs.jl with step_rk3! for every closure).
  • IMEX (2 × 8): step_imex!'s explicit part fuses buoyancy, Coriolis, the surface flux and the sponge (fused_sweep_cores.jl, against "never").
  • Open faces in the sound-proof cores (5 × 8): step 5 verified the open-face update after the sweep for the Boussinesq core only; new key builtin.anelastic_open_boundaries.
  • SimulationCouplings (2 × 5): step 2 made the obstacles a type parameter, step 5 added the IBM penalty term but none for the slab's floor flux, and with step 2's single term the penalty would have been booked at :tendencies. New generic Components.stage_sweep_term(c, d, Val(S)): the floor flux at :tendencies, the penalty at :body_force, each in its stage's place and budget context; the type parameter is the obstacles' type (with a Bool the penalty term's type was not inferred). fused_sweep_nearwall.jl (slab with obstacles, Euler and RK3, against "never"; a fresh SlabLand per run, a shared one is mutable state).
  • Urban site term (3 × 4): step 3's UrbanSiteTerm{S,Nothing} where {S} and UrbanSiteTerm methods of add_row! / add_tendency! were ambiguous for a periodic site; until step 4 the site never fused, so no run called them, and with step 4 every urban step threw and failed after its retries. Fixed (S<:UrbanSite); the urban tests in fused_sweep_reductions.jl and fused_sweep_post.jl caught it, and no ambiguity is left among the sweep interface's functions.
  • Order of prepare! (4 × 3, 5): the facade wall flux and the site's top-flux capture read the substage's νt in prepare!, which the SGS term's prepare! writes; the SGS term comes before the hook terms.

Test expectations made outdated by the combination (not weakened): the examples of unfused terms (SGS, buoyancy, Coriolis, the surface layer, couplings with obstacles) are fused now, so the tests check them fused against "never", and the unfused examples are a body_force! / post_predictor! function or the anelastic core's SGS; the dry CBL example fuses every substage ("all"), its unfused run and the "always" refusal use the anelastic core.

Tests: full suite 124 / 124 files, 16313 assertions, 0 failures (fused_sweep_sgs.jl 5832, fused_sweep_post.jl 1614, fused_sweep_pointwise.jl 893, fused_sweep_reductions.jl 823, fused_sweep.jl 753, fused_sweep_budgets.jl 679, fused_sweep_nearwall.jl 654, fused_sweep_cores.jl 581; urban_run.jl 42, urban_checks.jl 20). Berlin 100 m (berlin_100m_20250824.toml, copy with absolute driver paths, 12 threads, FFTW 12, measure planner): [loop_fusion.main] substages_fused = "all", not_fusable = [] (on issue/533-sweep-interface the same config: "none", 8 blockers). Fields after 50 steps against "never": max |Δu| 4.8e-7 m/s, |Δw| 1.5e-8 m/s, θ, v, qv identical in the Float32 output. Wall time of steps 2-50 (one run each, under the benchmark lock, one tile update inside): "if_possible" 0.270 and 0.250 s/step, "never" 0.364, issue/533-sweep-interface 0.340; about 26-31 % less than "never". A single 49-step window, not the 4-turn protocol above: an indication, the HPC benchmark (#566) measures it properly.

Still not fusable: the sound-proof cores' SGS, Coriolis, surface flux, sponge, open faces and post terms (step 2's and 4's terms are not wired into them); Urban.NestingCoupling (no term) and GeostrophicCoupling's nudging mode; by design a body_force! / post_predictor! function, another momentum advection, a LargeScale damping toward a 3-D target, step_imex!'s explicit SGS halves, budgets in the per-point form.

The tile surface update (#537)

Part of #531 (the Berlin step). The tiled surface's update (step_tiles!, every 60 s, so every 30 steps at Δt = 2 s) took 3.5 s at 1 thread and scaled 4.7× on 12. What was built:

  • No boxed closure locals. The profile showed the update was mostly not physics: solve_surface_layer's closures scales and F, and SoilColumn._phase_state's Heq and dHeq, assigned names the enclosing function also assigns, so Julia shared them in Core.Boxes. Every surface-layer solve allocated ~9 KB and ran its iteration by dynamic dispatch. Every layer evaluation of a soil column returned an uninferred tuple. The closures' locals are now local. The surface-layer fix reaches every surface that calls the solve, not only the tiles.
  • No vectors per column. The air of a column (ColumnAir) and its tiles' forcings (TileForcings, collected by parent_tile_forcings) are lazy vectors. soil_enthalpy and soil_ice_mass sum without a temporary. The Mualem normalisation f(S_c) is a field of VanGenuchtenMualem, not two powers per conductivity call. Per column: 64 KB → 1.3 KB allocated. What remains is the dynamic dispatch on the abstractly typed tiles and the mosaic's tile-flux vector.
  • A dynamic schedule for column launches. foreach_column! and column_sum take schedule = :dynamic (Backends.dynamic_columns!): guided self-scheduling from an atomic counter, each run half the remaining columns' share per thread. No machine constant. The partials are still folded in column order, so the totals are the same bits under either schedule and at any thread count. The tile update uses it: a Berlin column costs 2–60 µs, set by its number of soil tiles.

Measured (Ryzen AI 9 HX 370; Berlin berlin_100m_20250824.toml, 200 × 200 columns, FFTW estimate; every run under the shared bench lock; before = master 9053fe04; one update = the minimum of 4 after 2 steps):

threads1261224
one update, before3.524 s2.000 s1.030 s0.742 s0.662 s
after: boxes, allocations, static rows (680d5818)1.063 s0.420 s0.230 s0.198 s
after: + dynamic schedule1.497 s0.800 s0.301 s0.183 s0.143 s
speed-up2.4×2.5×3.4×4.1×4.6×
allocated per update2,882 → 50 MB

The update now scales 8.2× on 12 threads and 10.5× on 24, against 4.7× and 5.3× before. Berlin steps 32–61, which include one update (mean ms/step; minimum over 1 run at 1 thread, 3 runs at 2, 6 and 12):

threads12612
mean step, before656.4407.2282.5277.5
mean step, after585.2362.2261.8255.4
the step with the update, before → after4298 → 22582444 → 11701518 → 7041154 → 450
GC during the 30 steps, before → after (ms/step)8.8 → 1.613.0 → 2.013.1 → 1.9

Between updates the median step is unchanged within the noise (≈ 338 ms at 2 threads, ≈ 245 at 6 and 12). The mean falls by more than the amortised update (at 12 threads, 22 ms against 18.6) because the GC no longer collects the update's 2.9 GB of garbage during the steps after it. The fluxes, sources, u* and skin temperatures of two updates, and the area sums of the surface, are bit-identical to master's 1-thread run. On the final code this was checked at 1, 3, 4 and 12 threads (the 12- thread check by the review).

Bug the validation caught. A first version wrote a mosaic's tile fluxes into its own vector instead of a new one per step. validation/urban_checks.jl check 3 holds last_tile_fluxes of one step and steps on, and its held fluxes became the next step's (H 175.9 against −5.6 W m⁻²). The code was wrong, not the check, so the new vector per step is back. A new test in surface_tiles.jl guards it. The 680d5818 row of the table above was measured with that in-place write, so it slightly flatters that stage. Filling the new vector in a loop instead of a comprehension (cdd8b26e) was first credited with 1.69 → 1.50 s per update at 1 thread from one run each. Three interleaved runs each under the lock disagree: 1.489 / 1.496 / 1.508 s before and 1.482 / 1.475 / 1.479 s after (minimum of 4 updates per run), about 1 %, and 54.2 → 50.4 MB allocated per update. The first figure was noise. Nearly all of the 1-thread gain comes from the boxes and the lazy vectors.

Review. The PR gate runs at 1 thread, where a :dynamic launch takes the serial path, so a claiming bug would have passed it. dynamic_columns! takes ntasks. backends.jl runs 4 claimers that yield after every index on one thread, and checks that every index is claimed once, the guided run lengths (1:125, then 126:234 for 1000 indices) and the partials. The thread-invariance worker's columns section records a dynamic launch. Its subprocess level could not be run: on master the section already fails before that entry, at the aqchem Berlin `buildcase(parentdriver = "aeolus"with no parent domain), andflagshipfails the same way. A scratch copy of the entry gave the same rows and totals, bit for bit, at 1, 4 and 24 threads, and the same over 20 repeats in each process.backends.jl,componentsparent.jlandthread_invariance.jlpass at–threads 4and24`.

Deviations. The issue suspected rows of unequal land use. Once the GC spikes were gone, the per-row cost at 1 thread was 7.0–9.9 ms. The real imbalance is the static split itself: 50 blocks of 4 rows over 12 tasks, plus cores of unequal speed. Most of the gain came from the type instabilities, which the issue did not name. The staggered update (1/N of the columns per step) is left to the maintainer, as the issue proposed. The van Genuchten powers are now about half of the update (Richards sub-steps of the soil tiles).

Tests: surface_exchange.jl and soil_thermal.jl (no allocation in a solve, a slab's fluxes, a coupled soil step unfrozen and frozen, with its energy ledger), components_parent.jl (step_tiles! against a column-by-column reference over three surface steps of an uneven air), backends.jl (the dynamic schedule: every index once, totals equal to the static schedule's), surface_tiles.jl. validation/run.jl --only urban_checks.jl: 33 pass.

The late slowdown of the Berlin step (#535)

Diagnosed: the machine's package power limit, not the code. No code changed. The measurement protocol it calls for is in Benchmarks.

  • The question. The Berlin 100 m step (200 × 200 × 60, dt = 2 s, 12 threads) went from 287 to 480–510 ms over its first ~300 steps, with SGS doubling, at a fixed amount of work per step.

  • How it was measured. All timings were taken on master 9053fe04, FFTW estimate, one run at a time. A step! loop logged per step: wall, core, pressure and SGS time, GC time, bytes allocated, the APU package power (amdgpu hwmon PPT), Tctl and the mean clock. Every 30 steps it ran fixed-work canaries (an all-thread FMA chain and triad), and every 60 steps a subnormal census of the 166,446 float arrays reachable from the simulation. Two CLI runs had a 1 s sensor sampler running next to them.

  • Measured (12 threads, 540 steps, plain steps only; every 30th step, the tile update, is left out):

    stepsms/stepSGS mspackage powerTctl
    1–60254–27441–4329–30 W100–101 °C
    61–165274–34441–4925–28 W101 °C
    166–345427–64182–12415.0 W86–98 °C
    346–390253–26741–4528–30 W94–100 °C
    391–480275–37041–5425–29 W101 °C
    481–540508–538101–10715.0 W91–97 °C
    542–550, after 180 s idle237–25340–4234–47 W81–90 °C

    Classed by power: 299 ms/step boosted against 488 at the cap. Pressure 33 against ~60 ms/step, triad canary 40–50 against 26–36 GB/s. The CLI (300 steps) shows the same: 255–279 ms at 29.5–30.2 W, 319–376 ms at 22.7–27.5 W, 443–487 ms at 14.9–15.1 W. The issue's numbers are this 15 W phase.

  • The deciding evidence. At step 346 the late state runs at the early speed (253–267 ms) in the middle of the run, with nothing paused, as soon as the power returns to 28–30 W. A data-dependent cost could not do that. In the 15 W phase Tctl falls, so the limit is power, not temperature.

  • Ruled out:

    • Subnormals: 1 subnormal value in 126.5 M at every census, steps 1–301. With flush-to-zero on every thread from step 1: 288 ms boosted and 444 ms capped, the same slowdown. Switched on late at the same 46.7 W: 244 → 236 ms, inside the ±10 ms step-to-step scatter.
    • Allocations and GC: 7.4 MB/step from step 2 to 560, GC 0–2.5 ms per plain step with no trend (17–28 ms/step in the CLI, flat).
    • Data-dependent work and cache effects: excluded by the mid-run recovery.

  • Thread count. At 6 threads the package stays at about 24 W and never enters the 15 W phase: 280–324 ms/step over 450 steps (mean ~305), against a 12-thread mean of ~380 ms over 540 steps. A property of this laptop, not a constant for the code.

  • Consequence for #531 and #533. Their per-kernel tables (steps 32–61, right after the build) are boost-phase numbers. The overnight 0.604 s/step is the sustained mix of the phases. Fewer bytes per step also mean less power per step, which helps most in the capped phase.

  • Deviations from the issue's plan:

    • The late window was not rerun from a checkpoint on a cold machine. The mid-run recovery and the 180 s idle pause answer the same question without a restart.
    • The late window was profiled per component (pressure, SGS, the rest of the core) and not kernel by kernel with bench_roofline.jl: every component slows together, in step with the power.
    • Preheating with the light canary load for 240 s did not slow the first steps (248 ms): it draws less power than the step, so it does not use up the boost budget.

Phase 85 — Asynchronous and in-situ workflows

Asynchronous I/O, in-situ analysis and visualisation, in-transit compression, and streaming output so that a large run does not stall on disk. Deliverable: I/O no longer visible in the timing breakdown.

Phase 86 — Spectral-element backend completion

Finish the SEM backend: tensor-product operators, element-local mass matrices, matrix-free CG, curved elements, and p-adaptivity — giving high-order accuracy on genuinely complex geometry. Validated by: exponential convergence on smooth problems; the FV benchmark suite reproduced on aligned SEM meshes.

Phase 87 — Discontinuous Galerkin option

A DG variant for problems needing shock capturing or extreme hp-adaptivity, sharing the same physics modules. Validated by: shock-tube and compressible-turbulence cases.

Machine learning, applications, ecosystem

With a differentiable, validated, fast model in hand, the payoff milestone.

Phase 88 — Neural SGS closures

Train SGS models on filtered DNS and superdroplet reference data, with online (solver-in-the-loop) training via the Phase 74 adjoint, plus stability guarantees and physical-constraint enforcement. Validated by: a-posteriori skill on unseen flow regimes; stability over long integrations.

Phase 89 — Neural emulators for expensive physics

Emulate radiation, chemistry and microphysics with neural networks at 10²–10³× speed-up, with conservation constraints and a fallback to the reference solver when the emulator leaves its training distribution. Validated by: benchmark-suite skill preserved; measured speed-up.

Phase 90 — Differentiable-physics applications

End-to-end optimisation problems: optimal sensor placement, optimal urban-geometry design for ventilation and cooling, control of building systems, all via gradients through the solver. Deliverable: worked design-optimisation case studies.

Phase 91 — Foundation-model coupling

Use AI weather models (GraphCast, Pangu, Aurora, AIFS) as boundary forcing and as a background for assimilation, and study hybrid AI-physics forecasting at the urban scale. Validated by: forecast-skill comparison against reanalysis-driven runs.

Phase 92 — Wildfire coupling

Coupled fire-atmosphere modelling: fuel model, spread-rate parameterisation, fire-induced buoyancy and winds, smoke emission and its radiative feedback. Validated by: FireFlux experimental data; historical fire cases.

Phase 93 — Wind energy

Actuator-line and actuator-disc turbine models, wake interaction, wind-farm control, and terrain-induced flow effects. Validated by: wake profiles against wind-tunnel and field measurements; comparison against SOWFA.

Phase 94 — Urban climate services

Operational-grade workflows: heat-island mitigation scenario comparison, thermal-comfort indices (UTCI, PET, MRT), pedestrian wind comfort, ventilation-corridor analysis, and climate-change-scenario downscaling. Deliverable: a reproducible scenario-comparison report for a real city district.

Phase 95 — Air-quality nowcasting

Operational dispersion and air-quality forecasting with data assimilation of monitoring-station data, source attribution, and exposure mapping. Deliverable: a running nowcast for a test city, evaluated against withheld stations.

Phase 96 — Emergency response

Hazardous-release modelling: dense-gas dispersion, indoor-outdoor exchange, evacuation-relevant exposure fields, and backward source attribution from sensor detections. Validated by: the Prairie Grass, Kit Fox and Jack Rabbit release datasets.

Phase 97 — Aviation and contrails

Contrail formation and persistence, wake-vortex evolution, airport microclimate, and the contrail-avoidance optimisation problem. Validated by: contrail-persistence comparison against satellite observations.

Phase 98 — Coupled Earth-system components

Two-way coupling to Oceananigans.jl for air-sea interaction, to hydrological models for urban flooding, and to SpeedyWeather.jl / ClimaAtmos.jl for large-scale context — via a proper coupler with conservative flux exchange. Validated by: conservation across the coupling interface; sea-breeze and coastal cases.

Phase 99 — Ecosystem packages

AeolusAnalysis.jl (post-processing, spectra, budgets, conditional sampling), AeolusViz.jl (GPU volume rendering, web export), AeolusDA.jl, AeolusCases.jl (the benchmark library as a package), and AeolusCoupler.jl. Deliverable: registered packages with their own docs and tests.

Phase 100 — Community, documentation and science

A full theory monograph, API-stability guarantees with semantic versioning, contributor guide, tutorial series, JOSS and Geoscientific Model Development papers, participation in model-intercomparison projects, and a governance model. Deliverable: Aeolus as a citable, community-governed model.

Kilometre-scale regional and climate modelling

A future direction, not part of the decade plan above (tracking issue #289). Through Phase 100 Aeolus is a large-eddy model: grid spacings of metres to tens of metres over a few kilometres, with the mesoscale entering from outside (Phase 68). Phases 101–112 would make Aeolus itself a convection-permitting regional model at about 3 km, in the class of ICON-LAM, COSMO-CLM and REMO-NH, so that one code and one physics library cover the scales from a European domain down to a Berlin street canyon.

Why it sits here. Every piece of it depends on something earlier:

  • the cost needs MPI and GPU (Phases 80–87);
  • the driving data needs the reanalysis readers (Phase 67) and the nesting interface of Phase 68 (#58, and the Berlin nesting epic #246);
  • long-run conservation needs the full-state checkpointing of #245;
  • the grey-zone physics needs the turbulence closures (Phases 54–59) to blend into.

A 3 km domain the size of EURO-CORDEX is roughly 1500 × 1500 × 60 ≈ 1.4 × 10⁸ cells. On the present single-threaded solver one time step would take of order 10 s, and a 30-year run of order a decade of single-core wall time before any physics. This is an HPC-only capability.

What changes in kind, not degree. At 3 km the model no longer resolves the energetic boundary-layer eddies, so the LES closures stop being valid. The full troposphere and the orography enter the dynamics, and the domain is a piece of a sphere driven at its edges. The phases below address each of these. A phase is done by the same standard as every other milestone: implemented to the reference literature, validated, stable at production resolution.

Phase 101 — Compressible non-hydrostatic dynamical core

A fully compressible core for a deep atmosphere (about 30 km):

  • split-explicit acoustic substepping with a horizontally explicit, vertically implicit treatment (Wicker & Skamarock 2002; Klemp, Skamarock & Dudhia 2007);
  • the same physics interface as the Boussinesq and anelastic cores, so every physics module runs on all three.

Validated by: density current (Straka et al. 1993); inertia-gravity waves in a channel (Skamarock & Klemp 1994); warm bubble; a baroclinic-wave test in a channel against the published reference.

Phase 102 — Orography in the core

A terrain-following core on the existing TerrainFollowingGrid (Phase 24), using a smooth-level coordinate (SLEVE; Schär et al. 2002) with metric terms and controlled pressure-gradient errors over steep slopes. Cut cells (Phase 21) are the alternative for very steep relief.

Validated by: Schär et al. (2002) mountain-wave test; a resting atmosphere over steep idealised terrain (spurious wind below a stated bound); a flow-over-hill case against linear theory.

Phase 103 — Grids on the sphere: one abstraction, several grid types

Several grid types behind one grid abstraction, so that the dynamical core and every physics module run unchanged on any of them. The grids chosen for regional work must also be usable, later, for global models.

  • The abstraction. Operators are written against general curvilinear metric terms: cell areas and volumes, edge lengths, the local basis and its rotation, map factors, latitude for Coriolis, and deep-atmosphere radius factors. The topology is a set of logically rectangular panels with explicit connectivity, so that halo exchange crosses panel edges (with the vector-component rotation done there) as it later crosses MPI ranks (Phase 80). The existing Cartesian box becomes one panel with trivial metrics, so the urban LES is the special case, not a separate code path.
  • Rotated-pole latitude–longitude (one panel), the REMO and COSMO-CLM choice for regional climate. It is the primary regional grid, because it keeps the staggered C-grid stencils and the KernelAbstractions kernels of #291.
  • Conformal map projections (Lambert conformal, polar stereographic, Mercator; one panel each), as in WRF, through map factors.
  • Cubed sphere (six panels, equiangular gnomonic). Global. Its panels are structured, so the same stencils and kernels apply, with rotated halos at the cube edges. A regional domain can be a subset of panels or a single stretched panel (Schmidt transform), so regional and global runs share one grid family. The spectral-element backend (Phase 86) on curved elements gives a high-order path on the same grid; ClimaAtmos and HOMME use that approach.
  • Unstructured icosahedral or Voronoi grids (ICON, MPAS): an explicit later option, not part of this phase. It needs unstructured operators (no i/j/k stencils) and would most naturally come through the DG and SEM backends (Phases 86–87). The abstraction keeps the door open by not baking i/j/k indexing into the physics interfaces (column physics already works per column).

Validated by:

  • the Williamson et al. (1992) shallow-water test suite on the cubed sphere, which is global: solid-body advection over the poles and across panel edges, and steady geostrophic flow, with errors and convergence order quoted;
  • solid-body advection across the rotated pole, and geostrophic balance preserved on the rotated and projected grids;
  • a baroclinic wave (DCMIP-style) on the cubed sphere, with the regional subset against the global run;
  • the Cartesian box unchanged bit for bit when run through the abstraction.

Phase 104 — Lateral and top boundary driving

  • Davies (1976) relaxation zones at the lateral boundaries.
  • Upper-boundary damping toward the driver.
  • Optional spectral nudging of the large scales (von Storch et al. 2000).
  • Driving from ERA5 and from CMIP-class global model output, built on the boundary-data contract of the nesting epic (#246) and the real-world data readers (Phases 67–73).
  • Mass and energy consistency of the boundary fluxes.

Validated by: a perfect-model "big brother" experiment (Denis et al. 2002); driving drift and boundary noise quantified.

Phase 105 — Grey-zone boundary-layer turbulence

A scale-aware boundary-layer scheme, for example an eddy-diffusivity mass-flux (EDMF) TKE closure (Siebesma et al. 2007), blended by grid spacing with the LES turbulence closures (Honnert, Masson & Couvreux 2011; Boutle et al. 2014). The same model then runs continuously from LES to 3 km, without switching physics by hand.

Validated by: the resolution dependence of a convective boundary layer from 100 m to 3 km against coarse-grained LES; GABLS1 and a shallow-cumulus case (BOMEX) at mesoscale resolution.

Phase 106 — Convection and clouds at kilometre scale

  • Scale-aware shallow convection, and an optional deep-convection scheme for grids coarser than about 5 km.
  • Subgrid cloud cover from a statistical cloud scheme.
  • The cloud microphysics (two-moment, P3) and the aerosol–cloud coupling, at kilometre scale.

Validated by: an idealised supercell; radiative–convective equilibrium; the diurnal cycle of convective precipitation (timing and intensity) against radar.

Phase 107 — Land surface and hydrology for climate time scales

Multi-year soil hydrology with groundwater, frozen soil and snow, river routing, lakes, vegetation phenology, and land-use tiles at kilometre scale (SurfaceTiles), built on the land-surface modules.

Validated by: multi-year eddy-covariance sites (FLUXNET); river discharge at gauged catchments; snow-cover duration.

Phase 108 — Radiative forcing for climate

  • Greenhouse-gas concentration scenarios.
  • Aerosol climatologies, for example MACv2-SP (Stevens et al. 2017).
  • Ozone and solar variability.
  • Sea-surface temperature and sea ice from the driver or from the ocean mixed layer.
  • A radiation call frequency with a stated error.

Validated by: offline radiative-forcing comparison against line-by-line reference cases; top-of-atmosphere and surface energy budgets against satellite and surface-radiation-network climatologies.

Phase 109 — Long-run conservation and production output

  • Domain mass, water and energy budgets closed and monitored over decades.
  • Drift detection.
  • Bit-reproducible restarts (building on #245).
  • Output in the CORDEX data-request conventions, written by the in-package NetCDF writer.

Validated by: a 10-year run with stated budget closure and no unexplained drift.

Phase 110 — Convection-permitting evaluation

A multi-year ERA5-driven hindcast at about 3 km over Central Europe and the Alps, in the protocol of the CORDEX Flagship Pilot Study on convective phenomena (Coppola et al. 2020; Ban et al. 2021). It is evaluated against gridded observations, radar and hourly station precipitation, with emphasis on extremes, and set beside the published convection-permitting ensemble (COSMO-CLM, ICON, REMO, WRF).

Validated by: skill within the spread of that ensemble for the diurnal cycle, the seasonal precipitation climatology and hourly extremes.

Phase 111 — Scale-seamless nesting

Online multi-level nesting inside one Aeolus run, from the 3 km regional domain through about 1 km to the urban LES of the Berlin case. Physics changes consistently with grid spacing through Phase 105, and there is two-way coupling with the urban scale. The Cartesian urban child sits in the curved parent as a local tangent plane, with the interpolation between the two geometries (metric terms, vector rotation) done in the nesting operators. At about 1 km the tangent-plane error is negligible, but it is designed in and tested, not assumed.

Validated by: the urban-scale result against the offline-driven Berlin case (#246); conservation across all levels.

Phase 112 — Climate-change downscaling

  • Scenario runs driven by CMIP-class global models.
  • The pseudo-global-warming method (Schär et al. 1996).
  • Ensembles with the uncertainty machinery of Phases 74–79.
  • Urban climate projections, for example Berlin heat-wave statistics, from the full chain down to street scale.

Validated by: historical-period skill (Phase 110) and a documented uncertainty budget for the projected change.

Milestone. Aeolus is a convection-permitting regional climate model, evaluated against the published ensemble, that nests seamlessly down to its own urban LES.

Cross-cutting commitments

These apply to every phase above, not to any one of them:

  • Validation first. A phase starts by identifying its reference data and ends by reporting skill against it. No phase ships on "it runs and looks plausible".
  • No silent approximations. Every parameterisation documents its assumptions, its validity range, and what it omits. Where a cheaper option exists it is selectable, never the silent default.
  • Regression-tested. The benchmark suite (Phases 71–72) runs in CI from Phase 67 onward; earlier milestones add their validation cases to it as they land.
  • Performance budget. Each phase records its cost. A phase that makes the flagship run more than ~20 % slower needs either an optimisation plan or an opt-in flag.
  • Docs as first-class. Theory page, API reference, and a worked example per phase.

Honest assessment

This is a decade of work and it assumes sustained effort, domain expertise across meteorology, cloud physics, chemistry, turbulence and numerical analysis, and access to reference data and HPC resources. A solo contributor should expect to reach the end of Clouds and microphysics (Phase 45) in that window, not the end of Phase 100.

The ordering is the important part. Physics and numerical fidelity come first because everything downstream — assimilation, ML, applications — inherits their errors. Performance comes late deliberately: there is no value in efficiently computing the wrong answer, and optimising before the physics settles freezes interfaces that still need to change.

Kilometre-scale modelling changes what kind of model Aeolus is: from a large-eddy model with the mesoscale entering at its boundaries to a regional model that contains its own mesoscale. Projects of that kind (ICON, COSMO-CLM, REMO) are built and maintained by institutional teams over many years. It belongs on the roadmap because the payoff, one physics library from the regional domain down to the street canyon, is distinctive. It should be started only once real-world data (Phases 67–73) and performance (Phases 80–87) are shipped, and broken into issues only when it comes into view.

The kilometre-scale model is deliberately not a global climate model. But its grid abstraction (Phase 103) is designed so a global one stays possible: the cubed sphere is global from the start, and the panel and metric abstraction leaves room for unstructured grids later. Global context in the meantime comes from coupling to existing global models (Phase 98) and from lateral driving (Phase 104).
